\Gamma_{\mathsf{inf}}
be the set of those

\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
(\gamma,0)
is scheduled infinitely
many times during
\rho
. Since
\rho
is progressive, we know that for
some
k\geq 1
, each
e_{j}
with
k\geq j
only spawns threads from

\Gamma_{\mathsf{inf}}
. Now since
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j}\geq_{1}\mathbf{m}_{j}
, this
implies that for
j\geq k
,
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{j}
only contains threads
\gamma

(together with the context-switch number when they are produced)
such that
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathsf{inf}}
.

Therefore, we obtain a new run
\rho^{\prime}
of our DCPS by replacing each

e_{j}
by
e^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j\geq k
. Then, the resulting run is progressive,
because the additionally spawned threads all belong to

\Gamma_{\mathsf{inf}}
. Moreover,
\rho^{\prime}
is starving.

E.2.
Proof of Lemma
6.4

See
6.4

Proof.

If
\mathcal{A}
has a starving run
\rho
, then it also has a consistent
run by
Lemma

6.1
. Hence, there are a number

i\in[0,K]
, configurations
c_{1},c_{2},\ldots
and thread executions

e_{1},e_{2},\ldots
that produce
\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2},\ldots
such that the
conditions for a consistent run are satisfied. In particular, with

V_{t}=\{\mathbf{m}_{j}\mid\text{$e_{j}$ has type $t$}\}
, the tuple

\mathfrak{V}=(V_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{T}}
is
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
-consistent. Then, by definition of

\mathfrak{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
-consistency, the tuple
\mathfrak{m}=\alpha_{B}(\mathfrak{V})
is also

\mathfrak{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
-consistent. Therefore, the run
\rho
is
(i,\mathfrak{m})
-starving.

Conversely, suppose
\rho
is a shallow
(i,\mathfrak{m})
-starving run
for some
i\in[0,K]
and some
\mathfrak{S}
-consistent tuple

\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{P}([0,B]^{\Lambda})^{\mathcal{T}}
. Then we have configurations

c_{1},c_{2},\ldots
, and thread executions
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots
that
produce
\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2},\ldots
such that the conditions of

(i,\mathfrak{m})
-starvation are satisfied.

Let
V_{t}=\{\mathbf{m}_{j}\mid\text{$e_{j}$ has type $t$}\}
and

\mathfrak{V}=(V_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{T}}
. Since
\rho
is shallow, we know that
each
V_{t}
is a finite set. Moreover, since
\mathfrak{m}=\alpha_{B}(\mathfrak{V})
is

\mathfrak{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
-consistent,
Theorem

6.2
tells us that

\mathfrak{V}
is
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
-consistent as well. Thus,
\rho
is
consistent. Therefore
\mathcal{A}
also has a starving run by

Lemma

6.1
.

E.3.
Freezing
\mathsf{DCPS}

In this section we prove
Lemma

6.5
, which states that it is decidable whether a freezing
\mathsf{DCPS}

\mathcal{A}
has a
K
-context bounded progressive run, and if such a run exists then we can always assume it to be shallow. To this end we construct a
\mathsf{DCPS}

\mathcal{A}
that simulates
\mathcal{A}
in a way that preserves progressiveness and shallowness, while using at most
2K+1
context switches. The stated properties for freezing
\mathsf{DCPS}
then follow from our results on
\mathsf{DCPS}
(
Theorem

2.3
,
Lemma

4.3
, and
Corollary

4.5
).

Idea

The only differences between
\mathsf{DCPS}
and freezing
\mathsf{DCPS}
are the presence of frozen threads (marked with a top of stack symbol in
\Gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
) in configurations, and the rule
Unfreeze
. To simulate the former we just add
\Gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
to the stack alphabet and introduce a few new transition rules that spawn the initial frozen thread at the start of each run. To simulate
Unfreeze
for a rule
g\mapsto g^{\prime}\lhd\gamma\mathbin{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\gamma^{\prime}
, we follow these steps starting in global state
g
:

•

Resume a thread with
\gamma^{\prime}
as top of stack symbol, change it to
\gamma^{\prime\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
then swap out this thread.

•

Resume a thread with
\gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
as top of stack symbol, change it to
\gamma
and go to
g^{\prime}
with this thread staying active.

While these steps change the stack symbols correctly and make the right thread active, there are two things of note here: Firstly, if
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
then the very thread swapped out at the end of the first step could be resumed at the start of the second step, which is undesired behavior. To remedy this, we add a second copy of
\Gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
to our stack alphabet, which we call
\bar{\Gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
. We modify the simulation so that each time a thread with top of stack symbol in
\Gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
is swapped out in the first step, a thread with top of stack symbol in
\bar{\Gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
is resumed in the second step and vice-versa. Thus the two steps can no longer act on the very same thread, which fixes the issue.

Secondly,
freezing
a thread in the first step causes it to make a context switch, which does not match the specification of
Unfreeze
. However,
unfreezing
a thread makes it active and therefore adds a context switch once the thread is swapped out again. Thus, a thread can be frozen and unfrozen a total number of
K+1
times each in the freezing
\mathsf{DCPS}

\mathcal{A}
. In the
\mathsf{DCPS}

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
such a thread would then make
2K+2
context switches, with the last one happening right after it was active from being unfrozen the
(K+1)
th time. This means that the highest context switch number it reaches while being active is
2K+1
, which is exactly what we chose as the context switch bound for
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

With this increased context switch bound, we need to make sure that threads do not make more than
K+1
context switches in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, if they are resumed without being frozen. To this end we just artificially increase the context switch number by forcing an additional context switch every time a non-frozen thread is resumed. This mirrors the extra context switch caused by freezing, and therefore makes these threads behave correctly with a context switch bound of
2K+1
as well.

Formal construction

Let
\mathcal{A}=(G,\Gamma,\Delta,g_{0},\gamma_{0},\gamma_{f})
with
\Delta={\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}\cup{\Delta_{\mathsf{i}}}\cup{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}}\cup{\Delta_{\mathsf{t}}}\cup{\Delta_{\mathsf{u}}}
be a freezing
\mathsf{DCPS}
. We construct the
\mathsf{DCPS}

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=(G^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},g_{0}^{\prime},\gamma_{0}^{\prime})
, where:

•

\Gamma^{\prime}=\{\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\}\cup\Gamma\cup\bar{\Gamma}\cup\Gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\cup\bar{\Gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
with
\bar{\Gamma}=\{\bar{\gamma}|\gamma\in\Gamma\}
,
\Gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}=\{\gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}|\gamma\in\Gamma\}
, and
\bar{\Gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}=\{\bar{\gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}|\gamma\in\Gamma\}
.

•

G^{\prime}=G\cup\bar{G}\cup(G\times\Gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})\cup(G\times\bar{\Gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})
with
\bar{G}=\{\bar{g}|g\in G\}

•

\Delta^{\prime}={\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}^{\prime}\cup{\Delta_{\mathsf{i}}}^{\prime}\cup{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}}^{\prime}\cup{\Delta_{\mathsf{t}}}^{\prime}
consists of the following transition rules:

(1)

For the initial configuration:

(a)

g_{0}^{\prime}\mapsto g_{0}^{\prime}\lhd\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}}^{\prime}
.

(b)

g_{0}^{\prime}|\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow g_{0}^{\prime}|\gamma_{f}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\triangleright\gamma_{0}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}^{\prime}
.

(c)

g_{0}^{\prime}|\gamma_{f}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\mapsto g_{0}|\gamma_{f}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{i}}}^{\prime}
.

(2)

For each rule
g_{1}\mapsto g_{2}\lhd\gamma\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}}
:

(a)

g_{1}\mapsto\bar{g}_{2}\lhd\gamma\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}}^{\prime}
.

(b)

\bar{g}_{2}|\gamma\hookrightarrow\bar{g}_{2}|\bar{\gamma}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}^{\prime}
.

(c)

\bar{g}_{2}|\bar{\gamma}\mapsto\bar{g}_{2}|\bar{\gamma}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{i}}}^{\prime}
.

(d)

\bar{g}_{2}\mapsto g_{2}\lhd\bar{\gamma}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}}^{\prime}
.

(e)

g_{2}|\bar{\gamma}\hookrightarrow g_{2}|\gamma\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}^{\prime}
.

(3)

For each rule
g_{1}\mapsto g_{2}\lhd\gamma_{1}\mathbin{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\gamma_{2}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{u}}}
:

(a)

g_{1}\mapsto(g_{2},\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})\lhd\gamma_{2}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}}^{\prime}
.

(b)

(g_{2},\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})|\gamma_{2}\hookrightarrow(g_{2},\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})|\gamma_{2}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}^{\prime}
.

(c)

(g_{2},\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})|\gamma_{2}\mapsto(g_{2},\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})|\bar{\gamma}_{2}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{i}}}^{\prime}
.

(d)

(g_{2},\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})\mapsto(g_{2},\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})\lhd\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}}^{\prime}
.

(e)

(g_{2},\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})|\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\hookrightarrow g_{2}|\gamma_{1}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}^{\prime}
.

(4)

Furthermore, for each rule
g_{1}\mapsto g_{2}\lhd\gamma_{1}\mathbin{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\gamma_{2}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{u}}}
:

(a)

g_{1}\mapsto(g_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})\lhd\gamma_{2}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}}^{\prime}
.

(b)

(g_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})|\gamma_{2}\hookrightarrow(g_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})|\gamma_{2}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}^{\prime}
.

(c)

(g_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})|\gamma_{2}\mapsto(g_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})|\gamma_{2}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{i}}}^{\prime}
.

(d)

(g_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})\mapsto(g_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})\lhd\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}}^{\prime}
.

(e)

(g_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}})|\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\hookrightarrow g_{2}|\gamma_{1}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}^{\prime}
.

(5)

Finally,
\Delta^{\prime}
contains some unaltered rules of
\Delta
:

(a)

r\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}^{\prime}
for each rule
r\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}
.

(b)

r\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{i}}}^{\prime}
for each rule
r\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{i}}}
.

(c)

r\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{t}}}^{\prime}
for each rule
r\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{t}}}
.

Lemma E.1.

The freezing
\mathsf{DCPS}

\mathcal{A}
has a (shallow) progressive run that respects the context switch bound
K
iff the
\mathsf{DCPS}

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has a (shallow) progressive run that respects the context switch bound
2K+1
.

Proof.

From a given infinite run of
\mathcal{A}
we construct an infinite run of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
in such a way that progressiveness and shallowness are preserved. Then we argue that this construction can also be done backwards, starting with a run of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

Let
\rho
be an infinite run of
\mathcal{A}
. We construct the infinite run
\rho^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
by induction on the length of a prefix of
\rho
. Say
\rho
reaches the configuration
c=\langle g,t_{a},\mathbf{m}\rangle
after finitely many steps. Intuitively we want
\rho^{\prime}
to reach a configuration
c^{\prime}
that contains threads with the same stack contents as
c
, except for the top of stack symbol of the frozen thread, but with different context switch numbers. Any thread with cs-number
k
in
c
has cs-number
2k+1
in
c^{\prime}
if it was an active or frozen thread, and cs-number
2k
otherwise. Formally
c^{\prime}=\langle g,t_{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{m}\rangle
, where:

•

If
t_{a}=\#
then
t_{a}^{\prime}=\#
. If
t_{a}=(w,k)
then
t_{a}^{\prime}=(w,2k+1)
.

•

For all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
w\in\Gamma^{*}
:
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}(w,2k)=\mathbf{m}(w,k)
.

•

For all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
w\in\Gamma^{*}
,
\gamma\in\Gamma
:
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}(\gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w,2k+1)=\mathbf{m}(\gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w,k)
and
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}(\bar{\gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w,2k+1)=0
, or
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}(\bar{\gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w,2k+1)=\mathbf{m}(\gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w,k)
and
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}(\gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w,2k+1)=0
.

•

For all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
w\in\Gamma^{*}
,
\gamma\in\Gamma
:
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}(w,2k+1)=\mathbf{m}^{\prime}(\gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w,2k)=\mathbf{m}^{\prime}(\bar{\gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w,2k)=0
.

The second to last bullet point means that for a frozen thread in
c
with top of stack symbol
\gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
, the corresponding thread in
c^{\prime}
has either
\gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
or
\bar{\gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
as its top of stack symbol. The last bullet point simply ensures that
c^{\prime}
contains no additional threads (that do not correspond to a thread of
c
).

Now to construct
\rho^{\prime}
inductively. If
c=\langle g_{0},\#,{[\![(\gamma_{0},0)]\!]}+{[\![(\gamma_{f}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}},0)]\!]}\rangle
is the initial configuration of
\mathcal{A}
then we use the following transitions in
\rho^{\prime}
starting with the initial configuration of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\langle g_{0}^{\prime},\#,{[\![(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},0)]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\,\text{1a}\,}\langle g_{0}^{\prime},(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},0),\emptyset\rangle

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\text{1b}}\langle g_{0}^{\prime},(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},0),{[\![(\gamma_{0},0)]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\,\text{1c}\,}\langle g_{0},\#,{[\![(\gamma_{0},0)]\!]}+{[\![(\gamma_{f}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}},1)]\!]}\rangle=c^{\prime}.

Here and for all following transitions of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
we label each arrow with the corresponding transition rule of
\Delta^{\prime}
. Since we reach the correct
c^{\prime}
, this concludes the base case.

For the inductive case assume that we reach
c
after at least one step on
\rho
. We make a case distinction regarding the transition
\widetilde{c}\rightarrow c
or
\widetilde{c}\mapsto c
of
\rho
that reaches
c
:

Case
\langle g_{1},\#,\mathbf{m}+{[\![(\gamma w,i)]\!]}\rangle\mapsto\langle g_{2},(\gamma w,i),\mathbf{m}\rangle
due to
g_{1}\mapsto g_{2}\lhd\gamma\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}}
::

We add the following transitions to
\rho^{\prime}
, starting from the configuration obtained by applying the induction hypothesis to
\widetilde{c}
:

\displaystyle\langle g_{1},\#,\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![(\gamma w,2i)]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\,\text{2a}\,}\langle\bar{g}_{2},(\gamma w,2i),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\text{2b}}\langle\bar{g}_{2},(\bar{\gamma}w,2i),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\,\text{2c}\,}\langle\bar{g}_{2},\#,\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![(\bar{\gamma}w,2i+1))]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\,\text{2d}\,}\langle g_{2},(\bar{\gamma}w,2i+1),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\text{2e}}\langle g_{2},(\gamma w,2i+1),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\rangle=c^{\prime}.

Case
\langle g_{1},\#,\mathbf{m}+{[\![\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{1},i]\!]}+{[\![\gamma_{2}w_{2},j]\!]}\rangle\mapsto\langle g_{2},(\gamma_{1}w_{1},i),\mathbf{m}+{[\![\gamma_{2}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{2},j]\!]}\rangle
:

due to
g_{1}\mapsto g_{2}\lhd\gamma_{1}\mathbin{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\gamma_{2}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{u}}}
::

We again start from the configuration obtained by applying the induction hypothesis to
\widetilde{c}
. However, we need to make a case distinction based on the top of stack symbol of the thread in this configuration that corresponds to the frozen thread of
\widetilde{c}
. If this symbol is in
\Gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
, we add the following transitions to
\rho^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\langle g_{1},\#,\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{1},2i+1]\!]}+{[\![(\gamma_{2}w_{2},2j)]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\,\text{3a}\,}\langle(g_{2},\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}),(\gamma_{2}w_{2},2j),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{1},2i+1]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\text{3b}}\langle(g_{2},\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}),(\bar{\gamma}_{2}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{2},2j),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{1},2i+1]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\,\text{3c}\,}\langle(g_{2},\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}),\#,\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{1},2i+1]\!]}+{[\![(\bar{\gamma}_{2}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{2},2j+1)]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\,\text{3d}\,}\langle(g_{2},\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}),(\gamma_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{1},2i+1),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![(\bar{\gamma}_{2}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{2},2j+1)]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\text{3e}}\langle g_{2},(\gamma_{1}w_{1},2i+1),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![(\bar{\gamma}_{2}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{2},2j+1)]\!]}\rangle=c^{\prime}.

Otherwise, if the symbol is in
\bar{\Gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
, we add the following transitions to
\rho^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\langle g_{1},\#,\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{1},2i+1]\!]}+{[\![(\gamma_{2}w_{2},2j)]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\,\text{4a}\,}\langle(g_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}),(\gamma_{2}w_{2},2j),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{1},2i+1]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\text{4b}}\langle(g_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}),(\gamma_{2}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{2},2j),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{1},2i+1]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\,\text{4c}\,}\langle(g_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}),\#,\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{1},2i+1]\!]}+{[\![(\gamma_{2}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{2},2j+1)]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xmapsto{\,\text{4d}\,}\langle(g_{2},\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}),(\bar{\gamma}_{1}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{1},2i+1),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![(\gamma_{2}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{2},2j+1)]\!]}\rangle

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\text{4e}}\langle g_{2},(\gamma_{1}w_{1},2i+1),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![(\gamma_{2}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}w_{2},2j+1)]\!]}\rangle=c^{\prime}.

The remaining four cases use the same transition rules for both
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
, as the rules defined in (5) are ones already present in
\Delta
:

Case
\langle g_{1},(\gamma w,i),\mathbf{m}\rangle\rightarrow\langle g_{2},(w_{2}w_{1},i),\mathbf{m}\rangle
due to
g_{1}|\gamma\hookrightarrow g_{2}|w_{2}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}
::

\langle g_{1},(\gamma w,2i+1),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\rangle\xrightarrow{\text{5a}}\langle g_{2},(w_{2}w_{1},2i+1),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\rangle=c^{\prime}.

Case
\langle g_{1},(\gamma_{1}w_{1},i),\mathbf{m}\rangle\rightarrow\langle g_{2},(w_{2}w_{1},i),\mathbf{m}+{[\![(\gamma_{2},0)]\!]}\rangle
due to
g_{1}|\gamma_{1}\hookrightarrow g_{2}|w_{2}\triangleright\gamma_{2}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}
::

\langle g_{1},(\gamma_{1}w_{1},2i+1),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\rangle\xrightarrow{\text{5a}}\langle g_{2},(w_{2}w_{1},2i+1),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![(\gamma_{2},0)]\!]}\rangle=c^{\prime}.

Case
\langle g_{1},(\gamma w_{1},i),\mathbf{m}\rangle\mapsto\langle g_{2},\#,\mathbf{m}+{[\![(w_{2}w_{1},i+1)]\!]}\rangle
due to
g_{1}|\gamma\mapsto g_{2}|w_{2}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{i}}}
::

\langle g_{1},(\gamma w_{1},2i+1),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\rangle\xmapsto{\,\text{5b}\,}\langle g_{2},\#,\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+{[\![(w_{2}w_{1},2i+2)]\!]}\rangle=c^{\prime}.

Case
\langle g_{1},(\varepsilon,i),\mathbf{m}\rangle\mapsto\langle g_{2},\#,\mathbf{m}\rangle
due to
g_{1}\mapsto g_{2}\in{\Delta_{\mathsf{t}}}
::

\langle g_{1},(\varepsilon,2i+1),\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\rangle\xmapsto{\,\text{5c}\,}\langle g_{2},\#,\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\rangle=c^{\prime}.

Each
c^{\prime}
reached in any of the cases correctly corresponds to
c
as described above. Spawn boundedness is equivalent for
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
since corresponding segments where a thread is active make the same amount of spawns in both runs. Progressiveness is also equivalent: Every transition that makes a thread active in
\rho
(either by resuming or unfreezing) results in a corresponding thread becoming active in
\rho^{\prime}
. Conversely every two resume transitions due to rules from (2) in
\rho^{\prime}
correspond to a single resume transition in
\rho
, and each resume transition due to (3d) or (4d) corresponds to an unfreezing transition in
\rho
. Resume transitions due to (3a) or (4a) cause a thread to gain a top of stack symbol from
\Gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}\cup\bar{\Gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
, which can be the case for only one thread at a time. Thus, if
\rho^{\prime}
is progressive, this very thread has to be resumed, causing a later resume transition due to (3d) or (4d), which we already discussed. Finally, termination occurs at context switch number
k
in
\rho
iff it occurs at
2k+1
in
\rho^{\prime}
, which matches the relationship between the two context switch bounds for these runs.

For the other direction we start with an infinite run
\rho^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Observe that
\rho^{\prime}
decomposes into infixes that have the same form as the transition sequences obtained during the construction for the other direction. This is because for each of the alphabets
\bar{\Gamma}
,
\Gamma^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
, and
\bar{\Gamma}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}
there is always at most one thread that has a top of stack symbol in that alphabet; which causes the transition rules defined for
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
to not allow for any other types of behavior. Thus, we can do the previous construction backwards and obtain a run
\rho
of
\mathcal{A}
that has equivalent progressiveness and shallowness properties when compared to
\rho^{\prime}
.

E.4.
Detailed construction of
\mathcal{A}_{(i,\mathfrak{u})}

We construct a freezing DCPS

\mathcal{A}_{(i,\mathfrak{u})}=(G^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},g^{\prime}_{0},\gamma^{\prime}_{0},\gamma_{\dagger})
. Here,

g^{\prime}_{0}
is a fresh state and
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}
are fresh stack symbols, which
are both used for initialization. Moreover,
\gamma_{\dagger}
is a fresh stack
symbol that will be the top of stack of the initially frozen thread.

In addition to the stack of a thread in
\mathcal{A}
, a thread in

\mathcal{A}_{(i,\mathfrak{u})}
tracks some extra information

(t,j,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})
, where

•

t
is the type of the current thread execution,

•

j
is the number of
segments that have been completed,

•

\bar{\mathbf{m}}
is the guess for
\alpha_{B}(\mathbf{m})
,
where
\mathbf{m}
is the production of the thread execution,

•

\bar{\mathbf{n}}
is
\alpha_{B}(\mathbf{n})
, where

\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
is the multiset that has been produced
so far.

When a thread is inactive, this extra information is stored on its top
of stack symbol. For this, we need the new alphabet

\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma\cup\tilde{\Gamma}\cup\{\gamma^{\prime}_{0},\gamma_{\dagger}\}
, where

\tilde{\Gamma}=\{(\gamma,t,j,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})\mid\gamma\in\Gamma,~\text{$t$ is a type},~\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}}\in[0,B]^{\Lambda}\}.

While a thread is active, this extra information is stored in the
global state. This makes it easier to update it, e.g. when the stack
is popped. Moreover, we need a global state
\widehat{g}
for each

g
, which enforce that the information is transfered from the stack
to the global state. In order to execute the initially frozen thread with top-of-stack
\gamma_{\dagger}
,
we need global states
g^{\dagger,j}
for
g\in G
and
j\in[0,K]
.

Finally, we need special global states
g^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\in[0,K]
to
execute an initial helper thread with top-of-stack
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}
. Thus,
we have

G^{\prime}=G\cup\tilde{G}\cup\widehat{G}\cup\{g^{\prime}_{j},g^{\dagger,j}\mid j\in[0,K]\}
, where

\displaystyle\tilde{G}

\displaystyle=\{(g,t,j,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})\mid g\in G,~\text{$t$ is a type},~j\in[0,K],~\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}}\in[0,B]^{\Lambda}\},

\displaystyle\widehat{G}

\displaystyle=\{\widehat{g}\mid g\in G\}.

We now describe the transition rules of
\mathcal{A}_{(i,\mathfrak{u})}
.

Rules for initialization

We begin the description of a few rules that serve to initialize our
freezing DCPS. The initial configuration of our freezing DCPS is

\langle g^{\prime}_{0},\bot,{[\![(\gamma^{\prime}_{0},0),(\gamma_{\dagger}^{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}},0)]\!]}\rangle
. Since in our simulation, we
need that each thread in the bag is already annotated with its extra
information, we use a helper thread with top-of-stack
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}
to (i) spawn a thread simulating

\gamma_{0}
and also (ii) guess its extra information. Thus we have a
resume rule

g^{\prime}_{0}\mapsto g^{\prime}_{1}\lhd\gamma^{\prime}_{0}

to resume
(\gamma^{\prime}_{0},0)
. Then in this new thread, we spawn
(\gamma_{0},0)
, but with extra information. Thus, we have a creation rule

g^{\prime}_{0}|\gamma^{\prime}_{0}\hookrightarrow g^{\prime}_{1}|\gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rhd(\gamma_{0},t,0,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{0})

for every type
t
and
\bar{\mathbf{m}}\in[0,B]^{\Lambda}
. After this, our helper thread has to
complete
K
context-switches. This means, it has a interrupt rules

g^{\prime}_{j}|\gamma^{\prime}_{0}\mapsto g^{\prime}_{j+1}|\gamma^{\prime}_{0}

for
j\in[1,K-1]
and a rule to remove
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}
:

g^{\prime}_{K}|\gamma^{\prime}_{0}\hookrightarrow g^{\prime}_{K}|\varepsilon

Then, from
g^{\prime}_{K}
with an empty stack, we can only use the termination rule

g^{\prime}_{K}\mapsto g_{0}

which enters the global state that corresponds to the initial global state
g_{0}
of
\mathcal{A}
.

Creation rules

An internal action of a thread is simulated in the obious way. For each rule
g|\gamma\hookrightarrow g^{\prime}|w^{\prime}
, we have a rule

(g,t,j,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})|\gamma\hookrightarrow(g,t,j,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})|w^{\prime}

for every type
t
,
j\in[0,K]
, and
\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}}\in[0,B]^{\Lambda}
.

When our DCPS spawns a new thread, it immediately guesses its type
and production abstraction
\bar{\mathbf{m}}
. Moreover, it sets its
segment counter to
0
and sets
\bar{\mathbf{n}}
to

\mathbf{0}
. Hence, for every creation rule

g|\gamma\hookrightarrow g^{\prime}|w^{\prime}\rhd\gamma^{\prime}
, we have a rule

(g,t,j,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})|\gamma\hookrightarrow(g,t,j,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\alpha_{B}(\bar{\mathbf{n}}+{[\![(\gamma,j)]\!]}))|w^{\prime}\rhd(\gamma^{\prime},p^{\prime},0,\bar{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime},\mathbf{0})

for types
t,t^{\prime}
,
j\in[0,K]
, and

\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime},\bar{\mathbf{n}}\in[0,B]^{\Lambda}
. Note that
changing
\bar{\mathbf{n}}
to
\alpha_{B}(\bar{\mathbf{n}}+{[\![(\gamma,j)]\!]})

records that the current thread has spawned a thread
\gamma
in segment
j
.
Note that we perform this guessing of extra information with every
newly spawned thread, not just those that will be frozen and hence
yield the executions
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots
. Therefore, there is no
requirement here that
\bar{\mathbf{m}}
belong to
U_{p}
where

\mathfrak{u}=(U_{t})_{t\in\mathcal{T}}
.

Interruption rules

When we interrupt a thread, then its extra information is transferred from the global state to the top of stack and the segment counter
j
is incremented.
Thus, for every interrupt rule
g|\gamma\mapsto g^{\prime}|w^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}
, we write
w^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}w^{\prime\prime}
(recall that
1\leq|w^{\prime}|\leq 2
) and include rules

(g,t,j,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})|\gamma\mapsto g|(\gamma^{\prime\prime},t,j+1,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})w^{\prime\prime}

for every type
t
,
j\in[0,K-1]
, and
\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}}\in[0,B]^{\Lambda}
.

Resumption rules

In order to simulate a resumption rule
g\mapsto g^{\prime}\lhd\gamma
, we resume some thread with
\gamma
(and extra information) as top of stack. The transfer of the extra finformation cannot be done in the same step, so we have an additional state
\widehat{g^{\prime}}
in which this transfer is carried out. Hence, for every resumption rule
g\mapsto g^{\prime}\lhd\gamma
, we have rules

g\mapsto\widehat{g^{\prime}}\lhd(\gamma,p,j,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})

for each type
t
,
j\in[0,K]
,
\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}}\in[0,B]^{\Lambda}
. In
\widehat{g^{\prime}}
, we then transfer the extra information into the global state. Thus, we have

\widehat{g}|(\gamma,t,j,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})\hookrightarrow(g,t,j,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})|\gamma,

which exist for each
g\in G
,
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}}\in[0,B]^{\Lambda}
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
, and
j\in[0,K]
.

Termination rules

When we terminate a thread, we check that the two components
\bar{\mathbf{m}}
and
\bar{\mathbf{n}}
in the extra information match. Hence, for each termination rule
g\mapsto g^{\prime}
, we include a rule

(g,t,j,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{m}})\mapsto g^{\prime}

for each type
t
,
j\in[0,K]
, and
\bar{\mathbf{m}}\in[0,B]^{\Lambda}
.

Unfreezing rules

Using freezing and unfreezing, we make sure that there are thread
executions
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots
that satisfy the conditions of a

(i,\mathfrak{u})
-starving run. This works as follows. Initially, we have
the frozen thread
\gamma_{\dagger}
. To satisfy progressiveness, a run of

\mathcal{A}_{(i,\mathfrak{u})}
must at some point unfreeze (and switch to)

\gamma_{\dagger}
. During this unfreeze, we make sure hat there exists a
thread that can play the role of
e_{1}
.

Thus, to unfreeze
\gamma_{\dagger}
, we have to freeze a thread of some type

t
where
\bar{\mathbf{m}}
belongs to
U_{t}
:

g\mapsto g^{\dagger,0}\lhd\gamma_{\dagger}\mathbin{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}(\gamma,t,i,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})

for every
g\in G
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
,
\bar{\mathbf{m}}\in U_{p}
. The state
g^{\dagger,0}
is a copy of
g

in which we can only complete the execution of the
\gamma_{\dagger}
thread and then return to
g
.
This means, we have interrupt rules

g^{\dagger,j}|\gamma_{\dagger}\mapsto g^{\dagger,j+1}|\gamma_{\dagger}

for
j\in[0,K-1]
and
g\in G
, and resume rules

g^{\dagger,j}|\gamma_{\dagger}\mapsto g^{\dagger,j}|\gamma_{\dagger}

for
j\in[1,K]
and
g\in G
, a rule to empty the stack in the last segment:

g^{\dagger,K}|\gamma_{\dagger}\hookrightarrow g^{\dagger,K}|\varepsilon

for
g\in G
and finally a termination rule

g^{\dagger,K}\mapsto g

so that the simulation of
\mathcal{A}
can continue.

After this, the new frozen thread with top of stack

(\gamma,t,i,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})
has to be resumed (and thus
unfrozen) at some point. To make sure that at that point, there is a
thread that can play the role of
e_{2}
. Therefore, to unfreeze (and
thus resume) a thread with top of stack

(\gamma,t,i,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})
, we freeze a thread

(\gamma^{\prime},t^{\prime},i,\bar{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime},\bar{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})
with

\bar{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\in U_{t^{\prime}}
. Note that unfreezing always happens with
context-switch number
i
, because the executions
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots

have to be in their
i
-th segment in the configurations

c_{1},c_{2},\ldots
. Thus, we have

g\mapsto\widehat{g^{\prime}}\lhd(\gamma,t,i,\bar{\mathbf{m}},\bar{\mathbf{n}})\mathbin{\text{{\tiny{\char 94\relax}}}}(\gamma^{\prime},t^{\prime},i,\bar{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime},\bar{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime})

for each resume rule
g\mapsto g^{\prime}\lhd\gamma
, type
t
,

\bar{\mathbf{m}}\in U_{p}
, and
\bar{\mathbf{n}}\in[0,B]^{\Lambda}
, provided
that
g
is the state specified in
t
to be entered in the
i
-th
segment.

E.5.
Proof of Lemma
6.7

In this section, we prove
Lemma

6.7
. It will be
convenient to use a slightly modified definition of pushdown automata
for this.

Pushdown automata with output

If
\Gamma
is an alphabet, we define

\bar{\Gamma}=\{\bar{\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}
. Moreover, if

x=\bar{\gamma}
, then we define
\bar{x}=\gamma
. For a word

v\in(\Gamma\cup\bar{\Gamma})^{*}
,
v=v_{1}\cdots v_{n}
,

v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\Gamma\cup\bar{\Gamma}
, we set

\bar{v}=\bar{v_{n}}\cdots\bar{v_{1}}
. A
pushdown automaton with
output
is a tuple
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Gamma,\Lambda,E,q_{0},q_{f})
, where
Q
is a
finite set of
states
,
\Gamma
is its
stack alphabet
,

\Lambda
is its
output alphabet
,

E\subseteq Q\times(\Gamma\cup\bar{\Gamma}\cup\{\varepsilon\})\times{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}\times Q
is a finite set of
edges
,
q_{0}\in Q

is its
initial state
, and
F\subseteq Q
is its set of

final states
. A
configuration
of
\mathcal{A}
is a triple

(q,w,\mathbf{m})
with
q\in Q
,
w\in\Gamma^{*}
, and

\mathbf{m}\in{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
. For configurations
(q,w,\mathbf{m})
and

(q^{\prime},w^{\prime},\mathbf{m}^{\prime})
, we write
(q,w,\mathbf{m})\xrightarrow{}(q^{\prime},w^{\prime},\mathbf{m}^{\prime})
if
there is an edge
(q,u,\mathbf{n},q^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{A}
such that

\mathbf{m}^{\prime}=\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{n}
and (i) if
v=\varepsilon
, then
w^{\prime}=w
, (ii) if

v\in\Gamma
, then
w^{\prime}=wv
and (iii) if
v=\bar{\gamma}
for

\gamma\in\Gamma
, then
w=w^{\prime}\gamma
. By
\xrightarrow{}^{*}
, we denote the
reflexive transitive closure of
\xrightarrow{}
.

For a pushdown automata with output
\mathcal{A}
and a state
q
, we define

S_{\mathcal{A},q}=\{(w,\mathbf{m})\in\Gamma^{*}\times{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}\mid(q_{0},\varepsilon,\mathbf{0})\xrightarrow{}^{*}(q,w,\mathbf{m}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{}^{*}(q_{f},\varepsilon,\mathbf{m})~\text{for some $\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}$}\}

In other words,
S_{\mathcal{A},q}
collects those pairs
(w,\mathbf{m})
such that

\mathcal{A}
has a run that visits the state
q
with stack content
w
, and
the whole run outputs
\mathbf{m}
. Clearly,
Lemma

6.7

is a consequence of the following:

Lemma E.2.

Given a pushdown automaton with output
\mathcal{A}
and a state
q
, the
set
S_{\mathcal{A},q}
is effectively rational.

In the proof of
Lemma

E.2
, it will be convenient
to argue about the dual pushdown automaton. If
\mathcal{A}
is a pushdown
automaton with output, then its
dual automaton
, denoted

\bar{\mathcal{A}}
, is obtained from
\mathcal{A}
by changing each edge

(p,u,\mathbf{m},q)
into
(q,\bar{u},\mathbf{m},p)
, and switching the initial
and final state,
q_{0}
, and
q_{f}
. Moreover, we define

I_{\mathcal{A},q}=\{(w,\mathbf{m})\in\Gamma^{*}\times{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}\mid(q_{0},\varepsilon)\xrightarrow{}^{*}(q,w,\mathbf{m})\},

which is a one-sided version of
S_{\mathcal{A},q}
: In the right component,
we only collect the multiset output until we reach
q
and
w
.
However, using the dual automaton, we can construct
S_{\mathcal{A},q}
from the sets

I_{\mathcal{A},q}
. For subsets
S,T\subseteq\Gamma^{*}\times{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
, we define

S\otimes T=\{(w,\mathbf{m}_{1}+\mathbf{m}_{2})\in\Gamma^{*}\times{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}\mid(w,\mathbf{m}_{1})\in S,~(w,\mathbf{m}_{2})\in T\}.

Then clearly
S_{\mathcal{A},q}=I_{\mathcal{A},q}\otimes I_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},q}
. The next lemma follows using
a simple product construction.

Lemma E.3.

Given rational subsets

S,T\subseteq\Gamma^{*}\times{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
, the set
S\otimes T

is effectively rational.

Because of
S_{\mathcal{A},q}=I_{\mathcal{A},q}\otimes I_{\bar{\mathcal{A}},q}
,

Lemma

E.2
is a direct consequence of the
following.

Lemma E.4.

Given
\mathcal{A}
and
q
, the set
I_{\mathcal{A},q}
is effectively rational.

Proof.

Roughly speaking, we do the following. For each pair of states

p,p^{\prime}
, we look at the set
K_{p,p^{\prime}}\subseteq{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
of
outputs that can be produced in a computation that goes from
(p,w)

to
(p^{\prime},w)
without ever removing a letter from
w
. Note that this
set does not depend on
w
. Since
K_{p,p^{\prime}}
is semi-linear, there
is a finite automaton
\mathcal{B}_{p,p^{\prime}}
that can produce
K_{p,p^{\prime}}
. We
glue in between
p
and
p^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}
. In the resulting pushdown
automaton with output
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, we can then observe that every
configuration is reachable without ever performing a pop operation.
Therefore, removing all pop operations from
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and making
q

the only final state yields an automaton over

\Gamma^{*}\times{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
that accepts
I_{\mathcal{A},q}
.

Let us do this in detail. Let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Gamma,\Lambda,E,q_{0},q_{f})
be a
pushdown automaton with output. For each pair of states
p,p^{\prime}\in Q
,
we define

K_{p,p^{\prime}}=\{\mathbf{m}\in{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}\mid(p,\varepsilon,\mathbf{0})\xrightarrow{}^{*}(p^{\prime},\varepsilon,\mathbf{m})\}.

It
follows from Parikh’s theorem that each set
K_{p,p^{\prime}}
is
semi-linear. In particular, we can an automaton

\mathcal{B}_{p,p^{\prime}}
over
\Gamma^{*}\times{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
that accepts
\{\varepsilon\}\times K_{p,p^{\prime}}
.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be the pushdown automaton with output obtained from
\mathcal{A}

by glueing in, between any pair
p,p^{\prime}
of states, the automaton

\mathcal{B}_{p,p^{\prime}}
. Observe that for any reachable configuration

(p,w,\mathbf{m})
of
\mathcal{A}
, we can reach
(p,w,\mathbf{m})
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
without
using pop transitions: If in a run there is a transition that pops
some
\gamma
, we can replace the part of the run that pushes that

\gamma
, then performs other instructions, and finally pops

\gamma
, with a run in some
\mathcal{B}_{p,p^{\prime}}
. Conversely, any
configuration reachable in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
in a state that already exists in

Q
, is also reachable in
\mathcal{A}
.

Now let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
be the pushdown automaton with output obtained from

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
by removing all pop transitions. According to our
observation,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has the same set of reachable configurations in

Q
as
\mathcal{A}
. Since
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has no pop transitions, it is in fact an
automaton over
\Gamma^{*}\times{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
. Hence, if we make
q

the final state, we obtain an automaton over

\Gamma^{*}\times{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
for the set
I_{\mathcal{A},q}
.

E.6.
Proof of Lemma
6.11

See
6.11

Proof.

Suppose the rational subsets are given by automata

\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{k}
. By introducing intermediate states, we may
clearly assume that every edge in these automata either reads a
letter from
\Gamma
or a singleton multiset from
\Lambda
, meaning every
edge either belongs to
Q\times\Gamma\times\{\mathbf{0}\}\times Q
or is of the form

(p,\varepsilon,\mathbf{m},q)
with states
p,q
and
|\mathbf{m}|\leq 1
. Let
n

be an upper bound on the number of states of each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
,

i\in[1,k]
. Let
M
be the bound from
Lemma

6.10
and let

B=M(n+1)
.

Now let
\mathfrak{V}=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{k})
be a tuple of subsets of

{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
that admits an
\mathfrak{S}
-consistency witness

w\in\Gamma^{*}
. Moreover, let
\mathbf{m}\in{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
with

\alpha_{B}(\mathbf{m})\in V_{i}
. We prove the

Lemma
by constructing a word

\bar{w}\in\Gamma^{*}
with
w\leq_{\mathfrak{S}}\bar{w}
such that

\mathbf{m}\in S_{i}\mathord{\downarrow}_{\bar{w}}
. This implies the

Lemma
, because
\bar{w}
witnesses

\mathfrak{S}
-consistency of the tuple
\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}=(V^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,V^{\prime}_{k})
with

V^{\prime}_{i}=V_{i}\cup\{\mathbf{m}\}
and
V^{\prime}_{j}=V_{j}
for
j\neq i
.

Since
\alpha_{B}(\mathbf{m})\in V^{\prime}_{i}
and
w
is a
\mathfrak{S}
-consistency
witness, we know that
\alpha_{B}(\mathbf{m})\in S_{i}\mathord{\downarrow}_{w}
. This
means, there is a multiset
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
with

\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\geq_{1}\alpha_{B}(\mathbf{m})
and
(w,\mathbf{m}^{\prime})\in S_{i}
.

Observe that if we had
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\geq_{1}\mathbf{m}
, we could just choose

w^{\prime}=w
. Moreover, in those coordinates
c\in\Lambda
where

\mathbf{m}(c)<B
, we already know that
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}(c)\geq\mathbf{m}(c)
,
because
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}\geq\alpha_{B}(\mathbf{m})
. For those coordinates
c
with

\mathbf{m}(c)\geq B
, we will obtain
\bar{w}
by pumping an infix in
w
.

Let
c\in\Lambda
with
\mathbf{m}(c)\geq B
. Then

\mathbf{m}^{\prime}(c)\geq B
and therefore the pair
(w,\mathbf{m}^{\prime})
is accepted on
a run

q_{0}\xrightarrow{(u_{0},\mathbf{m}_{0})}q_{1}\xrightarrow{(u_{1},\mathbf{m}_{1})}\cdots\xrightarrow{(u_{B},\mathbf{m}_{B})}q_{B}\xrightarrow{(u_{B+1},\mathbf{m}_{B+1})}q_{B+1}

where
w=u_{0}\cdots u_{B+1}
,
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}=\mathbf{m}_{0}+\cdots+\mathbf{m}_{B+1}
, and

\mathbf{m}_{j}(c)\geq 1
for each
j=1,\ldots,B
. Since
B=Mn
, there is a state
p
of

\mathcal{A}_{i}
that appears at least
M
times in the sequence
q_{1},\ldots,q_{B}
.
This means, we have a run

q_{0}\xrightarrow{(v_{0},\mathbf{n}_{0})}p\xrightarrow{(v_{1},\mathbf{n}_{1})}p\xrightarrow{(v_{2},\mathbf{n}_{2})}\cdots p\xrightarrow{(v_{M},\mathbf{n}_{M})}q_{B+1}

with
w=v_{0}\cdots v_{M}
,
\mathbf{m}^{\prime}=\mathbf{n}_{0}+\cdots+\mathbf{n}_{M}
, and
\mathbf{n}_{j}(c)\geq 1
for each
j=1,\ldots,M-1
.

We claim that we can write
w=xyz
such that

w=xyz\leq_{\mathfrak{S}}xy^{\ell}z
for every
\ell
and that there is a run

q_{0}\xrightarrow{(x,\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{1})}p\xrightarrow{(y,\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{2})}p\xrightarrow{(z,\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{3})}q_{B+1}
with
\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{2}(c)\geq 1
.
We distinguish two cases.

(1)

First, suppose that there is a
j\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
with

v_{j}=\varepsilon
. Then we can choose this
v_{j}
as the
y
in
the decomposition
w=xyz
, which is clearly as desired.

(2)

Suppose that
v_{j}\neq\varepsilon
for every

j\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
. Then the
M
positions in the
decomposition
w=v_{0}\cdots v_{M}
are pairwise distinct and we can
apply
Lemma

6.10
. It clearly yields a decomposition of
w

as desired in our claim.

Hence, the claim holds in any case. If we now choose
\ell
high
enough, then we find a run of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
on

(xy^{\ell}z,\bar{\mathbf{m}}_{c})=(xy^{\ell}z,\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+\ell\cdot\bar{\mathbf{n}}_{2})
where

\bar{\mathbf{m}}_{c}(c)\geq\mathbf{m}(c)
and
\bar{\mathbf{m}}_{c}\geq_{1}\mathbf{m}^{\prime}
. If we
repeat this step for each
c\in\Lambda
with
\mathbf{m}(c)>B
, we arrive
at a word
\bar{w}
and a multiset
\bar{\mathbf{m}}
with

\mathbf{m}\leq_{1}\bar{\mathbf{m}}
and
w\leq_{\mathfrak{S}}\bar{w}
and

(\bar{w},\bar{\mathbf{m}})\in S_{i}
. This implies

\mathbf{m}\in S_{i}\mathord{\downarrow}_{\bar{w}}
.

E.7.
Proof of Proposition
6.9

See
6.9

Proof.

Suppose
\mathfrak{V}=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{k})
is a tuple of subsets of

{\mathbb{M}[\Lambda]}
. Clearly, if
\mathfrak{V}
is
\mathfrak{S}
-consistent, then
so is
\alpha_{B}(\mathfrak{V})
. The converse follows from

Lemma

6.11
: Since the sets
V_{1},\ldots,V_{k}
are
finite, we can start with
\alpha_{B}(\mathfrak{V})
and successively add each
multiset occurring in some
V_{i}
, without affecting

\mathfrak{S}
-consistency. Then we arrive at a
\mathfrak{S}
-consistent tuple

\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}=(V^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,V^{\prime}_{k})
with
V_{i}\subseteq V^{\prime}_{i}
for

i\in[1,k]
. In particular,
\mathfrak{V}
is
\mathfrak{S}
-consistent.
